## Page 1


Free information geometry and the model theory of
noncommutative stochastic processes
David Jekel
April 15, 2026
Abstract
We study entropy and optimal transport theory in the free probabilistic setting motivated by
the large-n theory of random tuples of matrices. We define a new version of free entropy χU
chron,
which is concave along geodesics in the corresponding Wasserstein space. Moreover, the heat
evolution satisfies the evolution variational inequality, which means that the heat evolution is the
Wasserstein gradient flow for entropy in the metric sense. It also has further desirable properties
such as a chain rule for iterated conditioning, and an expression in terms of stochastic control
problems. This entropy is defined using microstate spaces of matrix approximations with respect
to an expanded class of test functions called chronological formulas, which are constructed so as to
be closed under taking partial suprema and infima and application of a free heat semigroup. These
formulas are part of a novel framework for studying noncommutative filtrations and stochastic
processes as metric structures in the sense of continuous model theory.
1
Introduction
1.1
Motivation
The goal of this work is to study Wasserstein information geometry, the theory of optimal transport
and entropy, in the setting of free probability.
In classical Wasserstein geometry, one studies the
space of probability distributions on Rm, or more generally some manifold, equipped with a metric
coming from optimal transport theory.
The L2 Wasserstein distance of two probability measures
µ,ν ∈P(Cm) is the infimum of ∥X −Y∥L2 over random variables X ∼µ and Y ∼ν in some diffuse
probability space. The Wasserstein space can also be viewed as an infinite-dimensional manifold [55].
The entropy h(µ) = ∫−ρlog ρ is function on this manifold. The entropy (with the sign convention
chosen here) is concave along geodesics [57] [66, Corollary 17.19].
Moreover, the gradient flow of
entropy on the Wasserstein manifold is precisely given by the heat evolution of the measure [52].
These fundamental results unify many functional inequalities relating entropy, Fisher information,
and Wasserstein distance. Wasserstein geometry has had many applications in probability, statistics,
and data analysis. It has also inspired many generalizations, such as the development of gradient flow
in general metric spaces (see [2]). In fact, the free probability setting is a quite suitable test case for
the metric geometry perspective because, as explained below, it has very limited regularity compared
to the classical setting.
Free probability can be described as a theory of non-commuting random variables which bridges
random matrix theory and self-adjoint operator algebras. The space of random variables L∞(Ω,F,P)
is replaced with a von Neumann algebra M, and the expectation is replaced by a tracial state τ ∶M →
C. Independence is replaced by free independence, resulting in a free central limit theorem with the
Gaussian measure replaced by Wigner’s semicircular measure [67, 68]. The objects in free probability
also describe the large-n bulk behavior of several random matrices in many cases. A free semicircular
system (s1,...,sm) for instance models the large-n behavior of random matrices (S(n)
1
,...,S(n)
m ) from
the Gaussian unitary ensemble (GUE) [69].

## Page 2


Analogs of entropy in free probability were introduced by Voiculescu [70, 72] and soon had many
applications to the structure of free product von Neumann algebras (e.g. [71, 32, 35, 37, 36, 47]). A
free Wasserstein distance was defined by Biane and Voiculscu [12], and many functional inequalities
in information theory also have free analogs (e.g. [72, 38, 39, 18, 27, 64, 21]). Moreover, Guionnet
and Shlyahktenko [33] introduced a noncommutative analogs of optimal transport to show that von
Neumann algebra associated to free Gibbs law for convex potentials (a noncommutative analog of
strongly log-concave probability distributions) is isomorphic to that of a free semicircular family,
and the behavior of entropy and Wasserstein distance for free Gibbs law from convex potentials is
somewhat well understood (see [17, 41, 42, 19, 48]).
However, free information geometry in general is beset with long-standing open problems that
prevent a satisfying unified theory, in particular:
(1) Voiculescu defined two analogs χ and χ∗for entropy in [70] and [72]. While χ is defined in terms
of random matrix models, χ∗is defined by integrating the free Fisher information along the heat
flow, and it is unknown in general χ and χ∗are equal (see [73]). More generally, it is unclear
whether the heat flow is actually the gradient flow for free entropy, and whether the free Fisher
information is actually the norm of the gradient squared for entropy.
(2) The large deviations theory for several GUE matrices (S(n)
1
,...,S(n)
d
) has remained incomplete
since [11]. The rate function for the large deviations principle should be the free entropy. The
paper [11] proved an upper bound (implying χ ≤χ∗), and also gave a lower bound in the case of
elements given as a solution to certain class of free SDE.
(3) We do not know in general whether the classical Wasserstein distance associated to natural matrix
models converges to the free Wasserstein distance (and this is false for certain settings by [30,
§6.1] and [46, §4.4]).
A key piece of the puzzle that was not available until recently was a good free analog of Monge–
Kantorovich duality, which would be crucial for analyzing optimal couplings.
A more systematic
study of the free Wasserstein distance and Monge–Kantorovich duality in [30] also brought into focus
some of the obstructions that make the noncommutative setting inherently harder than the classical
case:
(1) There are many non-isomorphic tracial von Neumann algebras, including uncountably many that
cannot embed into each other [60]. As a consequence, the space of noncommutative laws (or
probability distributions) is not separable with respect to the Wasserstein distance [30, §5.5].
(2) For noncommutative probability distributions of bounded support, the Wasserstein distance gen-
erates a much stronger topology than the weak-∗topology [30, §5.4].
(3) Thanks to the far-reaching quantum complexity result MIP∗=RE [51], there are tracial von Neu-
mann algebras which do not admit any finite-dimensional approximations. Moreover, this also
implies that there are some noncommutative laws which do have finite-dimensional approxima-
tions, but such that the optimal coupling in the sense of Biane–Voiculescu distance does not admit
any finite-dimensional approximations [30, Corollary 5.14].
(4) It was also shown in [46, §4.4] that for certain noncommutative laws, one cannot construct random
matrix models that simultaneously have the correct behavior for entropy and for Wasserstein
distance (even after modifying the distance to only look at couplings that admit finite-dimensional
approximations).
In particular, unlike the classical case, two measures with finite entropy in the free setting can generate
very different von Neumann algebras, so that there is no hope of the optimal coupling being realized
by transport functions. However, the noncommutative Monge–Kantorovich duality shows that at least
in the interior of a geodesic, the associated von Neumann algebra is the same [30, Theorem 1.5].

## Page 3


In [44, 45, 46], we began to approach the problem of constructing a free information geometry by
expanding the class of test functions (hence also changing the notion of “noncommutative probability
distribution”). Let us motivate this in terms of geodesic concavity. In the classical setting, the geodesic
concavity of entropy on Rm is not hard to prove from optimal transport theory. Indeed, if (X0,X1)
are an optimal coupling of sufficiently regular probability measures, then there is a convex function φ
such that X1 = ∇φ(X0). The corresponding Wasserstein geodesic is the law µt of Xt = (id+t∇φ)(X0).
One can then compute h(Xt) using the change of variables formula for entropy:
h((id+t∇φ)(X0)) = h(X0) + ETrlog(id+tHφ(X0)),
and the latter is easily seen to be concave. One can also compute the derivative
d
dt∣
t=0
h((id+t∇φ)(X0)) = E∆φ(X0).
(1.1)
The problem in the free setting is that there is a large gap between the very low regularity that we
have for the functions in Monge–Kantorovich duality and the comparatively high regularity that we
need in order to use the change of variables for entropy.
The most basic scalar-valued functions in free probability are those of the form φ(x) = tr(p(x))
for some noncommutative polynomial p in several variables. By taking sums and products of such
functions, one obtains the algebra of trace polynomials. The derivatives of tr(p(x)) (in particular,
the gradient and Hessian) can be described in terms of Voiculescu’s cyclic gradient and free difference
quotient operators, and certainly one can make sense of the Laplacian and the trace of the log of the
Hessian.
Unfortunately, this is not a large enough class of functions to realize Monge–Kantorovich duality.
For this, we need to obtain a pair of convex functions φ and ψ such that
φ(x) + ψ(y) ≥⟨x,y⟩
with equality in the case of an optimal coupling of (µ0,µ1). It is natural to try to construct φ and ψ
via Legendre transforms of suitable other functions (see proof of Theorem 7.7). Thus, we might set
φ(x) = sup
y∈M m
sa
[⟨x,y⟩−η(y)]
where η is some trace polynomial (not necessarily convex). However, we immediately run into the
problem that the supremum might depend on how x sits inside the ambient von Neumann algebra
M, so it does not only depend on the noncommutative law of x. Hence, φ immediately falls out of
the class of trace polynomials (and its completion). This behavior is also strikingly different than
the classical setting: If I take a continuous function f(x,y) on R2m and a compact K ⊆Rm, then
g(x) = supy∈K f(x,y) will be continuous, and hence approximated by polynomials. In the case of
classical probability, or equivalently commutative von Neumann algebras, the supremum also does not
depend on the ambient algebra. In model-theoretic terms, this is because atomless classical probability
spaces admit quantifier elimination (see [10, Example 4.3] and [8, Fact 2.10]), and noncommutative
tracial von Neumann algebras usually do not [24, 26].
We can enlarge the space of test functions so that it is closed under partial suprema and infima
over operator-norm balls; this produces the class of formulas for tracial von Neumann algebras in
the sense of continuous model theory. Furthermore, in this larger class of test functions, we can find
convex functions that witness Monge–Kantorovich duality [45, Theorem 1.1]. However, we can no
longer apply the change of variables for entropy in this larger class of test functions, as it is not clear
how to define the Laplacian or free difference quotients. We would expect that the Laplacian should
satisfy

Therefore, in order for our class of test functions to be closed under both partial suprema and
infima and the heat semigroup, we want to study a filtration (Mt)t≥0 of von Neumann algebras and a
compatible Brownian motion zt (this means that Mt is a von Neumann subalgebra of M and Ms ⊆Mt
for s ≤t; zt is in Mt and zt −zs is freely independent of Ms for s ≤t). Each supremum or infimum will
have a specified domain which is an operator-norm ball one of the Mt’s. Moreover, we will assume
that all the sup and inf operations occur in chronological order, meaning that if s ≤t, a supremum
over Ms will always occur outside of a infimum over Mt, and so forth. Starting from scalar-valued
trace polynomials and repeatedly applying continuous functions, substitution of the Brownian motion
for some of the arguments, and partial suprema and infima in chronological order produces a class
of test functions we call chronological formulas. The associated analog of probability distributions is
called the chronological type.
Fortunately, the effort undertaken to define and study the space of chronological formulas (and its
completion) will be rewarded.
(1) We will obtain both a Monge–Kantorovich duality and an infinitesimal change-of-variables for-
mula for the associated free entropy χU
chron, and as a consequence show concavity of χU
chron along
Wasserstein geodesics.
(2) We will also show that the heat evolution of chronological types is the gradient flow for χU
chron in
the metric sense, that is, it satisfies the evolution variational inequality [59].
(3) We can define conditional entropy and obtain a chain rule for entropy under conditioning.
(4) We will make some progress in understanding the large deviations problem for several random
matrices; namely, we will prove a large deviations principle along each ultrafilter on N, with the
rate function given by a stochastic optimization problem, which can also be expressed as a limit
of chronological formulas.
Beyond the specific free probabilistic content, this article constitutes a case study of how Wasserstein
geometry interacts with ultraproducts, and in this connection we remark that Warren [75] has studied
the behavior of Wasserstein spaces under taking ultralimits of the underlying metric spaces. The
noncommutative setting is particularly challenging because of the model-theoretically wild behavior
4


## Page 5


of the limiting objects, which requires the development of new spaces and new techniques. Besides the
techniques from Voiculescu’s free entropy theory [70], this paper incorporates analysis of ultraproducts
and randomness from [8, 31], noncommutative Monge–Kantorovich duality from [30, 45], matrix
models for such optimal couplings from [46], noncommutative stochastic optimization problems from
[29, 28]
1.2
Main Results
We use the following terminology and conventions.
• A tracial von Neumann algebra is a von Neumann equipped with a specified faithful, normal,
tracial state.
• A noncommutative filtration is a family of tracial von Neumann algebra (Mt)t∈[0,∞] with Ms ⊆
Mt for s ≤t.
• We consider a free Brownian zt = (zt,j)j∈N whose operator real and imaginary parts form a free
semicircular family with common variance t, such that zt,j ∈Mt and zs,t = zt −zs is free from
Ms when s ≤t.
• We use boldface for tuples (despite that many works in continuous model theory use overline
instead).
Lowercase letters will be used for elements of general von Neumann algebras and
uppercase for matrices. We also write Mn for the n × n complex matrices.
• We work with general non-self-adjoint tuples.
Each non-self-adjoint variable could be expressed equivalently using two self-adjoint variables by taking
the operator real and imaginary parts. However, the non-self-adjoint version is convenient for applying
model theory of von Neumann algebras, since we take suprema and infima over operator norm balls
rather than sets of self-adjoint operators in the operator norm balls. Hence, the use of non-self-adjoint
variables makes the notation somewhat simpler, although it does not materially affect the results.
As described at the end of the previous section, we consider chronological formulas which are
generated recursively by the following operations:
• Starting with the real part of the trace of some noncommutative polynomial in the given variables
and in the increments of the process zs,t.
• Applying a continuous function f ∶Rk →R to some existing formulas φ1, . . . , φk.
• Applying the supremum or infimum over an operator-norm ball in M0 in one of the free variables,
which is thus no longer a free variable,
with the restriction that the suprema and infima occur in chronological order; see Definition 3.6. We
also study the space of chronologically definable predicates which is a natural completion of the space
of chronological formulas (Definition 3.28).
The space of chronological formulas in m free variables is denoted Fchron,m. The chronological
type tpM
chron(x) of an m-tuple x ∈M m
0
is the mapping Fchron,m →R given by sending a chronological
formula φ to its evaluation φM(x). We will define a variant of free entropy χU
chron(µ) where µ is the
chronological type of some m-tuple. The entropy χU
chron(µ) is defined in terms of the Lebesgue measure
or the Gaussian measure of certain spaces of matrix approximations, in the same way as Voiculescu’s
free entropy χ in [70]; we take the log-volume of the space of matrix approximations, divide by
n2, and take the limit along an ultrafilter U on N. However, the relationship between the matrix
approximations and the tuple x in the filtered noncommutative probability space is considerably
more complicated, as we explain in §1.3.
For the entropy for chronological types χU
chron, we are able to prove several foundational properties
for information geometry. This is the first time that such properties have been shown for any version
5


## Page 6


of multivariate free entropy. We first highlight the property of geodesic concavity with respect to the
L2-Wasserstein distance. As one might expect the Wasserstein distance of two types µ0 and µ1 is
defined as the minimal L2-distance ∥x0 −x1∥2 where x0 and x1 are m-tuples with types µ0 and µ1
respectively.
Theorem A (Geodesic concavity; see Theorem 7.11). Let x0 and x1 be m-tuples which form an
optimal coupling of chronological types µ0 and µ1. Let µt be the chronological type of (1 −t)x0 + tx1.
Then t ↦χU
chron(µt) is concave.
There are several important ingredients for this geodesic concavity theorem:
(1) We prove an analog of Monge–Kantorovich duality for the space of chronological types in Theorem
7.7, in much the same way as [45, Theorem 1.1].
(2) We prove an infinitesimal change of variables for free entropy in Theorem 6.25 (analogous to (1.1))
which works in general for chronologically definable predicates with limited regularity.
(3) Similar to [46, Lemma 4.9], we prove that there exist random matrix models X(n)
0
and X(n)
1
for
x0 and x1, such that X(n)
0
and X(n)
1
form a classical optimal coupling and also the normalized
classical entropy of X(n)
0
approximates χU
chron(x0); see Theorem 7.9. This result also heavily relies
on the Monge–Kantorovich duality in item (1).
The ingredients (1) and (2) are in fact sufficient to obtain concavity in the interior of the geodesic. For
0 < s < t < 1, we express convex functions associated to xs and xt in the Monge–Kantorovich duality
using Legendre transforms and inf-convolutions, show that the infinitesimal change of variables for
entropy can be applied to them, and give bounds for the Laplacian of the inf-convolution in terms of
that of the original function. The behavior at the endpoints of the geodesic requires special attention,
and this is handled using item (3) in the same way as in [46, Lemma 4.10].
Item (3) is also the key ingredient for proving the evolution variational inequality (EVI), which is a
metric version of the statement that the heat evolution gives the Wasserstein gradient flow of entropy
(see [2, 59]). Recall that the sign convention here is that χU
chron should be geodesically concave and
we consider the upward gradient flow. The version of the EVI that we use here is the second integral
characterization EVI0” from [59, Theorem 3.3] with λ = 0, applied to −χU
chron. The factor of 1/2 on
the left-hand side is because we express the heat semigroup in terms of Brownian motion, which puts
a factor of 1/2 in front of the Laplacian in the heat equation.
Theorem B (Evolution variational inequality; see Theorem 7.12). Let ν0 be the chronological type
of some m-tuple x0 in Qm and let νt be the chronological type of x0 + zt where zt is the Brownian
motion in Q. Let σ be another chronological type. Then we have the evolution variational inequality
1
2 [dW (νt,σ)2 −dW (νs,σ)2] ≤(t −s)[χU
chron(νt) −χU
chron(σ)] for 0 ≤s ≤t < ∞.
In fact, the evolution variational inequality implies geodesic concavity by [20, Theorem 3.2], so in
fact Theorem A is a consequence of Theorem B. Our proof of Theorem A, however, has independent
interest, since it gives a more specific description of the derivative of entropy along the geodesic in
Theorem 7.11 (2) and thereby demonstrates the viability of change of variables for free entropy in a
much more general setting.
Although ingredient (3) already holds in the setting of types in the language of tracial von Neumann
algebras, without any filtration, this is not enough for Theorem B to hold in this setting. The problem
is that we do not know whether the type of x0 + zt is uniquely determined by the type of x0, so that
νt is not well-defined to begin with. If there were a way to obtain the type of x0 + zt from the type of
x0 (specifically for the random matrix filtration Qt described in Proposition D below), then many of
the results in the paper could likely be proved in a simpler framework. However, this question seems
difficult and out of reach by current methods.
6


## Page 7


In this work, we also study the conditional version of the entropy. We define χU
chron(x ∣y), roughly
speaking, by fixing deterministic matrix approximations Y(n) for the tuple y, and then studying
the measure of the space of compatible matrix approximations for x (analogous to Shlyakhtenko’s
conditional microstate entropy in [63]). In fact, Theorems A and B hold in the more general setting
of conditional entropy and Wasserstein distance.
A key point is that for the setting of chronological types, the resulting conditional entropy is
independent of the choice of deterministic matrix approximations Y(n) (see Theorem 5.10). This is
the first time that this property has been proved for any version of conditional free entropy outside
of the very restricted case that y generates an amenable von Neumann algebra. The intuition is that
the chronological type has enough information to pin down the behavior of y exactly, so that two
different approximations cannot be chosen which would exhibit different behaviors in any way that
affects entropy (see the discussion around Theorem E below). As a consequence, we can obtain a
chain rule for entropy under iterated conditioning, while for previous versions of free entropy one can
only get inequalities (see [63, Proposition 2.5]).
Theorem C (Chain rule for entropy; see Theorem 5.15). Let x, y, and w be tuples of length m1 ∈N,
m2 ∈N, and m3 ∈N ∪{0,∞}. Then
χU
chron(x,y ∣w) = χU
chron(x ∣y,w) + χU
chron(y ∣w).
We also show another natural invariance property, namely, that if y and y′ generate the same von
Neumann algebra, then χU
chron(x ∣y) = χU
chron(x ∣y′), which was already known for other versions
of conditional free entropy; see [63, Proposition 2.15] and [49, Lemma 4.4]. In our setting, we can
upgrade the von Neumann algebra generated by y to the algebraic closure in the model-theoretic
sense. See §5.4 and compare also [45, §3] and [43, §4.6].
Remark 1.1. We warn the reader that for the chronological entropy to be finite, the chronological
type of (x,y) has to admit some matrix approximations as n →U, which in particular means that the
structure M has to be chronologically elementarily equivalent to the matrix ultraproduct Q described
in the next section (see Remark 5.11). However, this does not mean that χU
chron (or, similarly, the
entropy for full types in [44]) is useless for studying other von Neumann algebras. Rather, the notion
of types and the corresponding free entropy quantities are naturally suited for studying von Neumann
subalgebras inside Q. Any Connes-embeddable von Neumann algebra can be embedded in Q, usually
in many distinct ways. In general, spaces of types can also be viewed as spaces of embeddings into an
ultraproduct up to automorphic equivalence as in [5]. One can also use variational principles as in [44,
Lemma 4.3 and 4.7] to relate the more complicated entropy for types with the original free entropy
for noncommutative laws and entropy in the presence. See [47] for an application of these techniques.
1.3
Classical and noncommutative filtrations
In defining χU
chron, the main question is what to use for the space of matrix approximations for a
chronological type. In the original version of free entropy, matrix approximations are defined in terms
of moments; we look at matrix tuples X with trn(p(X)) ≈trM(p(x)) for noncommutative polynomials
p. In free entropy for types from [44], we consider formulas in the language of tracial von Neumann
algebras and demand that φMn(X) ≈φM(x). In both cases, the test functions are interpreted as
pointwise functions on Mn, so that there is no classical randomness involved in the definition of the
microstate space. This fits well with the concentration of measure phenomenon that applies to many
of the random matrix models that we care about. However, for chronological types, this approach
cannot be applied na¨ıvely since we need a filtration rather than a single von Neumann algebra in order
to evaluate a formula. Clearly, Mn does not have any nontrivial noncommutative filtration indexed
by t ≥0!
Instead, we work with the random matrix algebra L∞(Ω,G,P) ⊗Mn and consider a classical
filtration Gt of σ-algebras. We consider Brownian motions Z(n)
t,j
on Mn for t ≥0 and j ∈N, whose

we can obtain a truly noncommutative filtration. To see why a quotient is needed, consider for instance
that the increment zt,j −zs,j is not free from Ms with respect to the trace since zt,j −zs,j commutes
with the center Z(Ms) = ∏n→U L∞(Ω,Gt,P) (we have instead freeness with amalgamation over the
center). We therefore follow the method of [31] and take a quotient of M by a maximal ideal to obtain
a II1 factor. Specifically, let V be a pure state or character on the center Z(M), and consider the
ideal IV = {x ∈M ∶V ○EZ(M)[x∗x] = 0}. Let Q = M/IV and π ∶Q →M be the quotient map, and
Qt = π(Mt).
Proposition D (See Propositions 4.13 and 4.18). Let Mt and Qt be as above.
(1) (Qt)t≥0 is filtration of von Neumann algebras such that for s ≤t, the inclusion Qs ⊆Qt is elemen-
tary, and Qt is elementarily equivalent to the matrix ultraproduct ∏n→U Mn.

1.4
Organization
• §2 recalls relevant background and notation on von Neumann algebras, free probability, random
matrices, and model theory for metric structures.
• §3 sets up the language for a filtration of von Neumann algebras and an associated stochastic
process as metric structures. In particular, it defines the chronological formulas and a model-
theoretic version of stationarity.
• §4 applies the setup of §3 to quotients Q of a random matrix ultraproduct M as in (1.3). We
use the techniques of [31] to relate chronological formulas evaluated in Q to analogous objects
in M. We thus define the finite-dimensional approximations Λ(n)
φ
and prove Proposition D.
• §5 introduces the version of free entropy for chronological types, with respect to Gaussian mea-
sure. We prove Theorem E to express the entropy in terms of stochastic control problems. We
then show Theorem C on the chain rule, establish invariance of χU
chron(x ∣y) with respect to y,
and conclude with Corollary F on large deviations.
• §6 introduces entropy for chronological types with respect to the Lebesgue measure.
After
developing basic real analytic results for chronologically definable predicates and their finite-
dimensional approximations, we define limsup and liminf versions of the Laplacian and prove
the infinitesimal change of variables for entropy.
• §7 begins the proper study of information geometry. We define a Wasserstein distance for a
space of conditional chronological types. We prove a version of Monge–Kantorovich duality. We
prove an extension theorem for random matrix models of optimal couplings. We then conclude by
proving Theorem A on geodesic concavity and Theorem B on the evolution variational inequality.

important aspects of information geometry and functional inequalities, especially during his visit to
Copenhagen. Kohei Suzuki highlighted the problem of proving evolution variational inequalities in
discussions on March 22-24, 2026 at the conference “Entropy and random matrices” at CIRM in
Marseille.

Preliminaries
2.1
Von Neumann algebras
In this paper, a tracial von Neumann algebra, or equivalently tracial W∗-algebra refers to a finite von
Neumann algebras with a specified tracial state. We recommend [40] for an introduction to the topic,
as well as the following standard reference books [53, 22, 62, 65, 13, 76].
Tracial von Neumann algebras: A von Neumann algebra on a Hilbert space H is a unital
∗-subalgebra of B(H) which is closed in the weak operator topology (WOT) or equivalently closed
in the strong operator topology (SOT). Sakai [62] gave an abstract characterization of von Neumann
algebras as C∗-algebras which are also dual spaces as Banach spaces (C∗-algebra in turn are abstractly
characterized as Banach ∗-algebras satisfying ∥x∗x∥= ∥x∥2). A von Neumann algebra thus always has
a weak-∗topology, and this coincides with the σ-WOT, which is the WOT for M acting on H⊕∞.
A tracial von Neumann algebra is a pair M = (M,τ) where M is a von Neumann algebra and
τ ∶M →C is a faithful normal tracial state, that is, a linear map satisfying
• positivity: τ(x∗x) ≥0 for all x ∈M
• unitality: τ(1) = 1
• traciality: τ(xy) = τ(yx) for x,y ∈A
• faithfulness: τ(x∗x) = 0 implies x = 0 for x ∈A.
• weak-∗continuity: τ ∶M →C is weak-∗continuous.
We will often denote τ by trM for consistency with the model-theoretic notation introduced below.
We also denote by DM
r
the operator-norm ball of radius r.
We also use the following notations:
• Z(M) denotes the center of M.
• Msa denotes the set of self-adjoint elements.
• DM
r
denotes the r-ball in M with respect to operator norm.
Real/imaginary and polar decompositions: We will make frequent use of the operator real
and imaginary parts: For x ∈M, write Re(x) = (x + x∗)/2 and Im(x) = (x −x∗)/2i. Thus, Re(x)
and Im(x) are self-adjoint and x = Re(x) + iIm(x). We also recall that ∣x∣is defined as (x∗x)1/2. In
any tracial von Neumann algebra, one can write express x using a polar decomposition of the form
x = u∣x∣for some unitary u (not necessarily unique).
noncommutative Lp spaces: Let (M,τ) be a tracial von Neumann algebra. For m ∈N and
x,y ∈M m, denote
∥x∥p = ∥x∥τ,p =

where a plain ∥⋅∥always denotes the operator norm, and

## Page 12


(In this paper, inner products are assumed to be linear in the right coordinate.) We recall that M
can be completed with respect to ∥⋅∥p into a noncommutative Lp space Lp(M) (the dependence on τ
is suppressed in the notation). The adjoint operation x ↦x∗always extends isometrically to Lp(M).
The analogs of H¨older’s inequality and Lp-Lp/(p−1)-duality also hold for tracial von Neumann algebra.
In this paper, we will mostly use L2(M) and sometimes L1(M).
Embeddings and conditional expectations: An embedding or inclusion of tracial von Neu-
mann algebras always refers to a trace-preserving ∗-homomorphism, which is automatically injective
because of faithfulness of the trace. Given a tracial von Neumann algebra (M,τ) and a von Neumann
subalgebra (N,τ∣N), the orthogonal projection L2(M) →L2(N,τ∣N) restricts to a map EN ∶M →N,
and EN is contractive with respect to ∥⋅∥p for all p ∈[1,∞]. We refer to EN as the conditional expec-
tation from M onto N. The map EN is also a conditional expectation in the C∗-algebraic sense; it is
a completely positive N-N bimodule map with EN∣N = idN. We point out that the choice of trace is
necessary in order to uniquely determine EN, even though the choice of trace is often suppressed in
the notation.
Functional calculus: Von Neumann algebras are always closed under application of Borel func-
tional calculus and so in particular continuous functional calculus. Thus, for every normal operator
x in a tracial von Neumann algebra (M,τ), there is an associated spectral measure µ with respect to
the trace, given by
∫C f dµ = τ(f(x)) for f ∈C(C);
µ is a Borel probability measure supported on the spectrum of x, and in particular if x is self-adjoint
then µ ∈P(R). An important fact for us is that the application of a Lipschitz function on R by
functional calculus will be Lipschitz on L2(M)sa. (Beware, however, that it is not Lipschitz with
respect to operator norm.) This can be considered a folklore result in free probability or in operator
theory, but we include the proof for the reader’s convenience.
Lemma 2.1. Let f ∶R →R be an L-Lipschitz function. Let x and y be self-adjoint elements in a
tracial von Neumann algebra (M,τ). Then
∥f(x) −f(y)∥2 ≤L∥x −y∥2.
Proof. Assume that x and y are bounded in operator norm by some constant R > 0. Suppose that
f is polynomial.
Let ∂f be the free difference quotient of f; ∂is defined as the linear operator
on C[t] →C[t] ⊗C[t], such that when ∂[tk] = tj ⊗tk−1−j. Recall that there is a unique C∗-tensor

C([−R,R]) ⊗C([−R,R]) ≅C([−R,R]2) in the natural way. Hence, ∂[tk] viewed as an element of
C([−R,R]) ⊗C([−R,R]) corresponds to the two-variable function

∥∂f∥C([−R,R])⊗C([−R,R]) = ∥f(s) −f(t)

Now it is straightforward to show (using smooth curoffs and convolution) that if f is L-Lipschitz on
R, then f can be uniformly approximated on [−R,R] by polynomials fk which are also L-Lipschitz on
[−R,R]. We have fk(x) →f(x) and fk(y) →f(y) in operator norm, hence also ∥⋅∥2, and the asserted
inequality follows.
Truncation: The following truncation trick will be useful for our study of suprema and infima
over operator norm balls.
Lemma 2.2. Let (M,τ) be a tracial von Neumann algebra and x ∈M. Let R > 0. Let FR ∶R →R be
the function
FR(t) = min(max(t,−R),R).

Proof. (1) Note that {x′ ∶∥x′∥≤R} is a closed convex set in the Hilbert space L2(M,τ).
(2) Since the R-ball is invariant under left multiplication by unitaries, u∗x′ is the closest point to
∣x∣. Hence, it suffices to show that if x ≥0, then FR(x) is the closest point to x. Let ∥y∥≤R. Let
p = 1(R,∞)(x) and note that (x −FR(x))p = x −FR(x). Then
Re⟨FR(x) −y,x −FR(x)⟩= τ[(FR(x) −Re(y))(x −FR(x))]
= τ[(FR(x) −Re(y))p(x −FR(x))p]
= τ[p(FR(x) −Re(y))p(x −FR(x))].
Now p(FR(x) −Re(y))p = Rp −pRe(y)p ≥0 and x −FR(x) ≥0. Hence, the trace of the product is
nonegative, and so Re⟨FR(x) −y,x −FR(x)⟩≥0 for all y in the R-ball, which implies that FR(x) is
the closest point.
(3) Again, it suffices to consider the case when x is positive. Since t−FR(t) vanishes on [0,R], we
have
t2 −FR(t)2 = (t + FR(t))(t −FR(t)) ≥2R(t −FR(t)).
Evaluating on x and taking the trace, we see that
τ(x2) −τ(FR(x)2) ≥2Rτ(x −FR(x)) = 2R∥x −FR(x)∥1.
Ultraproducts: We briefly recall the definition of ultrafilters and ultraproducts of tracial von
Neumann algebras. For background, see [15, Appendix A], [16, §2], [3, §5.4], [30, §5.3].
An ultrafilter U on the natural numbers N is a collection of subsets U which is closed under
supersets and finite intersections, such that ∅/∈U for every A ⊆N, either A or Ac is in U. For each
n ∈N, there is an associated principal ultrafilter {A ⊆N ∶n ∈A}. We are primarily concerned with the
non-principal or free ultrafilters on N.
If U is an ultrafilter on N, we say that a set A is U-large if A ∈U. Similarly, a property is said to
hold for U-many n if the set of n that satisfies this property is an element of U. Let U be an ultrafilter
on N, and let (xn)n∈N be a sequence in some topological space. We say that limn→U xn = x if for every
neighborhood O of x, we have {n ∶xn ∈O} ∈U. If U is an ultrafilter on N and xn is a sequence in a
compact Hausdorff space, then limn→U xn exists and is unique.
We remark that ultrafilters on N are in bijection with characters on ℓ∞(N); here “character” refers
to a multiplicative linear functional ℓ∞(N) →C, or equivalently a pure state on ℓ∞(N) as a C∗-algebra,
where the character φ is given by φ(f) = limn→U f(n). The space of ultrafilters can thus be identified
with the space of characters on ℓ∞(N) or the Stone-ˇCech compactification of N.

that supn∥xn∥< ∞, which is a C∗-algebra. Let

2.2
Free probability and random matrices
Here we recall standard definitions and background on free probability and random matrices. See also
[74, 4, 58].
Free independence: Let (M,τ) be a tracial von Neumann algebra. We say that ∗-subalgebras
(Ai)i∈I are freely independent if whenever i1, . . . , ik ∈I with ij ≤ij+1, whenever aj ∈Aij for j = 1,
. . . , k, we have
τ[(a1 −τ(a1))...(ak −τ(ak))] = 0.
Similarly, elements are said to be freely independent if the ∗-algebras that they generate are freely
independent.
Semicircular and circular operators: A standard semicircular operator is a self-adjoint opera-
tor x in a tracial von Neumann algebra whose spectral measure is the Wigner semicircular distribution
1
2π
√
4 −t21[−2,2](t)dt. A standard semicircular family is a tuple (x1,...,xm) where x1, . . . , xm are
freely independent standard semicircular elements. A standard circular family in this paper will be a
family (z1,...,zm) such that (Re(z1),Im(z1),...,Re(zm),Im(zm)) are a standard semicircular fam-
ily. Beware that many authors define a free circular operator as (x+ iy)/
√
2 rather than x+iy, where
x and y are free semicirculars. We do not include the factor of 1/
√
2 because we want a simpler
conversion between the self-adjoint and non-self-adjoint cases, and because we want the resulting con-
ventions around free entropy for non-self-adjoint operators to match those of [44]. Note that ∥x + iy∥
thus becomes 2
√
2 rather than 2.
Filtrations and free Brownian motion: A noncommutative filtration is a family (Mt)t≥0 of
tracial von Neumann subalgebras of some (M,τ) such that if s ≤t, then Ms ⊆Mt. We also adopt the
convention that M∞= M. An adapted process α ∶[0,t0] →L2(M,τ) is a Bochner-measurable map
such that αt ∈L2(Mt,τ) for each t ≥0.
For m ∈N ∪{∞}, an m-variable (non-self-adjoint) free Brownian motion compatible with the
filtration (Mt) is a family of operators (zt,j)t≥0,j≤m such that
• For each t ≥0 and j ∈N, we have zt,j ∈Mt (i.e. t ↦zt,j is adapted).
• For each s < t, the family (zt,j −zs,j)j≤m is freely independent of Ms.
• For each s < t, the family (t −s)−1/2(zt,j −zs,j)j≤m is a standard circular family.
14


## Page 15


The filtration generated by the Brownian motion is the filtration (Nt)t≥0 where Nt is the von Neumann
algebra generated by (zs,j)s≤t,j≤m.
Free Gaussian functor: One of the main ways to construct a free Brownian motion is Voiculescu’s
free Gaussian functor [67, §3] [74, §2.6]. Given a real Hilbert space HR and its complexification H,
the functor produces a von Neumann algebra M(H) and a family of operators X(h) for h ∈HR such
that
• x(h) is ∥h∥times a standard semicircular.
• If H1, . . . , Hk are orthogonal subspaces of H, then the corresponding families of operators are
freely independent.
Moreover, any isometry H →H′ yields an trace-preserving ∗-homomorphism from M(H) to M(H′);
similarly, an autmorphism of H produces an automorphism of M(H). See [74, Theorem 2.6.2].
In particular, by taking H = L2([0,∞);Rm) and xt,j = x(1[0,t]ej), we obtain a self-adjoint free
Brownian motion. We can adapt the construction to the non-self-adjoint case, by taking the free
Gaussian functor applied to HR ⊕HR and setting z(h) = x(h ⊕0) + ix(0 ⊕h) for h ∈HR. Then with
L2([0,∞);Rm) we obtain a non-self-adjoint free Brownian motion.
Matrix algebras: We denote by Mn the n × n complex matrices and trn the normalized trace
(1/n)Trn. We equip Mm
n with the inner product
⟨X,Y⟩trn =
m
∑
j=1
trn(X∗
j Yj),
where trn = (1/n)Trn is the normalized trace; we also denote the corresponding normalized Hilbert-
Schmidt norm by ∥⋅∥trn.
As a complex inner-product space Mm
n may be transformed by a linear
isometry to Cm. By the Lebesgue measure on Mm
n , we mean the Lebesgue measure on Cm transported
by such an isometry, which is independent of the particular choice of isometry. Note that many authors
identify Mn with Cn2 entrywise to obtain Lebesgue measure, but our choice of Lebesgue measure differs
by a factor depending on n since we use trn rather than Trn to define the inner product. See e.g. [49,
§3.3] for further discussion of the normalization.
Gaussian random matrices and Brownian motion: A random matrix tuple is a random
variable on some classical probability space (Ω,G,P) which takes values in Mm
n .
A GUE (Gaussian unitary ensemble) random matrix is a random element of (Mn)sa with prob-
ability density (2πn2)−n2/2 exp(−n2∥X∥2
2/2) with respect to the Lebesgue measure on (Mn)sa with
respect to the normalized inner product ⟨⋅,⋅⟩trn. A Ginibre random matrix is a random element of
Mn with probability density (2πn2)−n2 exp(−n2∥X∥2
2/2) on Mn with respect to Lebesgue measure;
note that the real and imaginary parts are independent GUE matrices. Similar to the case of circular
operators, our convention differs by a factor of
√
2 from that of many other works.
Similarly, GUE and Ginibre families are m-tuples of independent GUE and Ginibre matrices re-
spectively. A Brownian motion on Mm
n will refer to a family (Zt,j)t≥0,j≤m with independent increments
such that (t −s)−1/2(Zt,j −Zs,j)j≤m is a Ginibre family for each s < t. Such a Brownian motion will is
said to be compatible with some filtration Gt of σ-algebras on the classical probability space (Ω,G,P)
if (Zs,j)s≤t,j≤m is Gt-measurable for each t > 0 (that is, (Zt,j)t≥0 is progressively measurable), and
(Zt,j −Zs,j)j≤m is independent of Gs for each s < t.
Asymptotic freeness: Voiculescu showed that GUE random matrices are asymptotically freely
independent of deterministic matrices. The same applies of course to Ginibre matrices and Brownian
motion on Mm
n . Various statements of the asymptotic freeness theorem can be found in [69, Theorem
2.2], [4, Theorem 5.4.2], [58, Chapter 4, Theorem 5], from which it is not hard to extract the following
statement.

2.3
Model theory
Next, let us sketch the setup of continuous model theory, or model theory for metric structures [9, 10].
We will follow the treatment in [25] which introduces “domains of quantification” rather than “sorts.”
Classical model theory deals with first-order statements: one starts with basic predicates associated
to the class of objects under consideration (for example, “xy = z” in the setting of groups), and then
16


## Page 17


builds new statements from them by applying quantifiers “for all” and “there exists” and connectives
like “and”, “or”, and “if/then.” In particular, each predicate takes the value true or false for any
given inputs. In continuous model theory, the predicates will take real values rather than true/false
values. For instance, the relation “=” which takes values true or false will be replaced by the metric
d which outputs a real-valued distance between two points.
Just as in classical logic, a particular language comes with function symbols and relation (or pred-
icate) symbols. In the case of groups, for instance, there is a binary function symbol for multiplication
and a 0-ary function, i.e. a constant, for the identity. In the case of ∗-algebras over C, one needs
symbols for addition, multiplication, adjoint, and scalar multiplication. While the function symbols
encode operations from M n into M for each structure M, the relation symbols take inputs from M
and output true/false in the discrete setting, and real values in the metric setting.
When the structures are metric spaces, the operations associated to the structures should also
be assumed to be continuous and globally bounded. In fact, there should be a uniform bound and
modulus of continuity independent of the particular structure, so that we will be able to construct
ultraproducts of the structures. This leads to another annoyance in the setting of Banach spaces,
C∗-algebras, von Neumann algebras, and the like, namely, the operations are not always globally
bounded or uniformly continuous on the whole space. We therefore dissect the space into a family of
domains of quantification (often the family of balls of radius r for nonnegative integers or rationals or
reals r) on which the operations are bounded and uniformly continuous. When we apply a supremum
or infimum, it will always be over one of these domains, to ensure that the result remains finite;
for instance, we may take supy∈D1 φ(x,y) where D1 is the unit ball and φ is some formula that is
already defined. If one wants to avoid using multiple domains, this can sometimes be done by suitably
modifying the collection of function symbols in order to phrase everything in terms of the unit ball
[7, Proposition 29.4]; however, this is not convenient for our purposes here since we want to reason
about globally defined functions.
Languages: A metric language L consists of:
• A set D whose elements are called domains of quantification.
• For each D,D′ ∈D an assigned constant CD,D′.
• A countably infinite set of variable symbols for each sort S. We denote the variables by (xi)i∈N.
• A set of function symbols, each with an assigned arity m ∈N.
• For each function symbol f and for every D = (D1,...,Dm) ∈Dm, an assigned Df,D ∈D (repre-
senting a range bound), and assigned moduli of continuity ωf,D,1, . . . , ωf,D,m. (Here “modulus
of coninuity” means a continuous increasing, zero-preserving function [0,∞) →[0,∞)).
• A set of relation symbols, each with an assigned arity n ∈N.
• A special binary relation symbol d for the metric.
• For each relation symbol R and for every D = (D1,...,Dm) ∈Dm, an assigned bound NR,D ∈
[0,∞) and assigned moduli of continuity ωR,D,1, . . . , ωR,D,m.
Structures: Given a language L, an L-structure M is a metric space M with an object assigned
to each symbol in L, called the interpretation of that symbol, in the following manner:
• Each domain of quantification D ∈DS is assigned a subset DM ⊆M, such that DM is complete
for each D, and M = ⋃D∈DS DM, and supx∈D,y∈D′ dM(x,y) ≤CD,D′.
• Each function symbol f of arity m is interpreted as a function fM ∶M m →M. Moreover, for
each D = (D1,...,Dn) ∈DS1 ×⋅⋅⋅×DSn, the function f M maps DM
1 ×⋅⋅⋅×DM
n into DM
f,D. Finally,
f M restricted to DM
1
× ⋅⋅⋅× DM
n
is uniformly continuous in the ith variable with modulus of
continuity (less than or equal to) ωf,D,i.

• Each relation symbol R of arity m is interpreted as a function RM ∶M m →R. Moreover, for each
D = (D1,...,Dm) ∈Dm, f M is bounded by NR,D on DM
1

m and uniformly continuous
in the ith argument with modulus of continuity of ωR,D,i.
Language for tracial von Neumann algebras: Next, we recall the language Ltr of tracial W∗-
algebras together with how each symbol will be interpreted for a given tracial von Neumann algebra,
as formulated in [25].
• Domains of quantification {Dr}r∈(0,∞), to be interpreted as the operator norm balls of radius r
in M.
• The metric symbol d, to be interpreted as the metric induced by the noncommutative L2-norm
∥⋅∥2,τ.
• A binary function symbol +, to be interpreted as addition.
• A binary function symbol ⋅, to be interpreted as multiplication.
• A unary function symbol ∗, to be interpreted as the adjoint operation.
• For each λ ∈C, a unary function symbol, to be interpreted as multiplication by λ.
• Function symbols of arity 0 (in other words constants) 0 and 1, to be interpreted as additive
and multiplicative identity elements.
• Two unary relation symbols Retr and Imtr, to be interpreted the real and imaginary parts of
the trace τ.
• For technical reasons (see [25, Proposition 3.2] and [34, p. 93]), we also introduce for each d-
variable noncommutative polynomial p and n ∈N a d-variable function symbol tp representing
the evaluation of p, along with the appropriate range bounds Ntp,r between Mp and Mp + 1/n,
where Mp is the supremum of ∥p(X1,...,Xd)∥over all (X1,...,Xd) in a tracial W∗-algebra M.
Each function and relation symbol is assigned range bounds and moduli of continuity that one
would expect, e.g. multiplication is supposed to map Dr × Dr′ into Drr′ with ω⋅
(Dr,D′r),1(t) = r′t
and ω⋅
(Dr,D′r),2 = rt. Although not every Ltr-structure comes from a tracial W∗-algebra, one can for-
mulate axioms in the language such that any structure satisfying these axioms comes from a tracial
W∗-algebra [25, §3.2].
Syntax: We now briefly recall terms, formulas, sentences, types, and theories. See [45, §2.3-2.4]
for more detail. Fix a set of variables X. In the following definitions, we write L-terms, L-formulas,
etc., but we will also refer to these objects simply as terms, formulas, etc. if the language L is clear
from context.
L-terms in X are expressions formed recursively by composition of the function symbols in L
applied to variables in X. For each L-structure M, the interpretation of a term t(x1,...,xm) is the
function M m →M obtained by composing the interpretation of the function symbols in M. For
instance, a term in the language of tracial von Neumann algebras is an expression in the ∗-algebra
operations, such as ((x1 + x2)x3 + x∗
4) + 3x1(x3x2) where x1, x2, . . . are variables in X. Although the
∗-algebra axioms are not assumed to hold in the definition of terms, every term will coincide with a
∗-polynomial when evaluated in an actual ∗-algebra.
L-formulas in X are expressions defined recursively as follows:
• Basic formulas have the form r(t1(x),...,tk(x)) where r is a k-ary relation symbol from L and
t1, . . . , tk are terms in variables x = (x1,...,xm) from X.
• Connectives: If φ1, . . . , φk are formulas in x = (x1,...,xm) and f ∶Rk →R is continuous, then
f(φ1,...,φk) is a formula.
18


## Page 19


• Quantifiers: If φ(x,y) is a formula where x1,...,xm,y ∈X are distinct, and if D ∈D, then
ψ1(x) = supy∈D φ(x,y) and ψ2(x) = infy∈D φ(x,y) are formulas.
Quantifier-free formulas are defined recursively using only the basic formulas and connectives. For
instance, for tracial von Neumann algebras, trace polynomials can be expressed using quantifier-free
formulas.
In a formula, the free variables are the variables which are not bound to any quantifier, and we
usually denote a formula as a function of the free variables. In general, a variable could have one
occurrence that is bound to a quantifier and one occurrence which is free, but by relabeling variables
we can assume that each quantifier in the formula has a unique variable associated to it. We also
denote by Fm the set of formulas in m free variables x1, . . . , xm.
Here not every free variable
is required to appear in the formula in a nontrivial way; that is, every formula in a subset of the
variables x1, . . . , xm is also an element of Fm.
Evaluation of formulas: Given an L-structure M and a formula φ(x), the evaluation or in-
terpretation of φ in M is the function φM ∶M m →R defined by plugging in inputs x1, . . . , xm
from M, evaluating each term and relation symbol in M, and evaluating each expression supy∈D
as the actual supremum over y ∈DM. We remark that if φ is a formula in m free variables and
D = (D1,...,Dm) ∈Dm, then φM is uniformly continuous on DM
1
× ⋅⋅⋅× DM
m with a modulus of
continuity that is independent of the particular L-structure M. This is easily seen by induction on
the complexity of the formulas [9, Theorem 3.5].
Sentences, theories, and axiomatization: A L-sentence is a formula with no free variables.
Thus, the evaluation of a sentence in M is a real number associated to M. An L-theory is a collection
of L-sentences. An L-structure M is said to satisfy a theory T, if φM = 0 for all φ ∈T (one also says
that M is a model of T or M ⊧T). A class C of L-structures is said to be axiomatizable if there is
a theory T such that C is the class of L-structures satisfying T. For instance, the class of tracial von
Neumann algebras and the class of II1 factors are axiomatizable in Ltr by [25]. Similarly, a property
is said to be axiomatizable if the class of structures with that property is axiomatizable.
Elementary equivalence and embeddings: Two L-structures M1 and M2 are said to be
elementarily equivalent if φM1 = φM2 for all L-sentences φ. An inclusion M1 ⊆M2 is said to be
elementary if for every formula φ in m variables and x ∈(M1)m, we have φM2(x) = φM1(x).
Types and definable predicates: Let m ∈N ∪{∞}. Given an L-structure M and x ∈M m, the
full type tpM(x) of x is the map Fm →R sending φ to φM(x). We denote by Sm(T) the set of types
tpM(x) of tuples from L-structures satisfying T. If D = (D1,...,Dm) is a tuple from D, then SD(T)
will denote the set of types associated to tuples from DM
1
× ⋅⋅⋅× DM
m and L-structures satisfying T.
We equip SD(T) with the weak-∗topology from the space of linear functionals on Fm. We equip
Sm(T) with the topology such that O is open in Sm(T) if and only if O ∩SD(T) is open in SD(T)
for each D.
Definable predicates are a certain completion of the space of formulas that are dual to full types.
As motivation, note that by definition of the weak-∗topology, every formula φ defines a continuous
function on SD(T), which is also a compact Hausdorff space (see [9, Proposition 8.6]). The continuous
functions given by formulas are a subalgebra of C(SD(T);R) that separates points and contiains 1,
and hence they are dense in C(SD(T);R). The norm of C(SD(T);R) corresponds to
∥φ∥D,T = sup
M⊧T

∣φM(x)∣.
It is therefore natural to study the completion of Fm with respect to the family of seminorms ∥⋅∥D,T
for D ∈Dm. The elements in the completion will be called definable predicates in m variables relative
to T. By [45, Lemma 2.16], every element of C(SD(T);R) agrees with some definable predicate. In
this paper, we will use (µ,φ) to denote the pairing of a type µ with a definable predicate φ, namely,
(µ,φ) = φM(x) when tpM(x) = µ.

3
Filtered NC probability spaces as metric stuctures
3.1
Setup of the language
The goal of this section is to set up the study of filtered NC probability spaces and adapted processes
as metric structures in the sense of §2.3 (for terminology on NC filtrations, see §2.2). Throughout, we
consider a process zt = (zt,j)j∈N for t ≥0 which is not necessarily self-adjoint, and we usually work in
terms the increments zs,t,j = zt,j −zs,j for s < t and j ∈N.
Due to the nature of the metric language for von Neumann algebras, we want to assume that
zs,t,j lies in an operator-norm ball Dr(s,t) where r(s,t) is independent of the particular structure.
Because we are concerned with circular Brownian motion, we take r(s,t) be some number larger
than 23/2(t −s)1/2. For concreteness and for convenience in the random matrix arguments, we take
r(s,t) = 6(t −s)1/2. Of course, if one would like to study a different process which is still continuous
in operator norm, one can adjust the constants accordingly, but we simply use 6(t −s)1/2 throughout
this paper.
Definition 3.1. The language Lproc is defined as follows:
• For each t ∈[0,∞] and r ∈[0,∞), there is an associated domain Dt,r.
• There is a binary function symbol + which maps Dt1,r1 ×Dt2,r2 into Dmax(t1,t2),r1+r2 for each t1,
t2, r1, and r2.
• There is a binary function symbol ⋅which maps Dt1,r1 × Dt2,r2 into Dmax(t1,t2),r1r2 for each t1,
t2, r1, and r2.
• There is a unary function symbol ∗which maps Dt,r into Dt,r for each t and r.
• For each λ ∈C, there is a unary function symbol λ that maps Dt,r into Dt,∣λ∣r for each t and r.
• There is a constant symbol 1 ∈D0,1.
• For 0 ≤s ≤t < ∞and j ∈N, there is a constant symbol zs,t,j ∈Dt,3(t−s)1/2.
• There is a predicate symbol d for the distance, which maps Dt1,r1 × Dt2,r2 into [0,r1 + r2].
• There are predicate symbols Retr and Imtr for the real and imaginary parts of the trace, which
map Dt,r into [−r,r].
We now consider how Lproc-structures relate with Ltr structures and with tracial von Neumann
algebras.
Definition 3.2. Let M be an Lproc-structure. For t ∈[0,∞], denote by Mt the Ltr-structure obtained
by considering only the domains (Dt,r)r∈[0,∞) and ignoring the constant symbols zs,t,j. Moreover, let
Mt = ⋃r>0 Dt,r as a set.
Definition 3.3. Let Tfilt be the set of axioms in Lproc described as follows:

(3) For each t1 ≤t2 ≤t3 < ∞, we have d(zt1,t2,j + zt2,t3,j,zt1,t3,j) = 0, which expresses that zt1,t2,j +
zt2,t3,j = zt1,t3,j.
The following lemma is immediate from the fact Ttr axiomatizes tracial von Neumann algebras.
Lemma 3.4. If M is an Lproc-structure satisfying Tfilt, then M∞is a tracial von Neumann algebra
and Mt is a von Neumann subalgebra for each t ∈[0,∞], with Mt1 ⊆Mt2 for t1 < t2.
We close the section with important notation that will be used throughout the paper.

3.2
Chronological formulas and stationarity
For our results in §4.2, we must restrict to formulas where the quantifiers appear in chronological
order, meaning that if s < t, each sup or inf over Ds,r occurs to the left (or outside) of any sup or
inf over Dt,r′ in the formula (for r,r′ ∈(0,∞)). Hence, in the remainder of the paper, we will mostly
work with these chronological formulas.
Definition 3.6 (Chronological formulas). For t ∈[0,∞), define a set of Lchron-formulas Fchron(t)
recursively as follows:
(1) Every basic formula is in Fchron(t) for all t.
(2) If φ1, . . . , φk ∈Fchron(t) and f ∶Rk →R is continuous, then f(φ1,...,φk) ∈Fchron(t).
(3) If φ(x,y) ∈Fchron(t) and s ≤t and r > 0, then supy∈Dr,s φ(x,y) and infy∈Dr,s φ(x,y) are in
Fchron(s).
We then set Fchron = ⋃t∈[0,∞) Fchron(t), and refer to this as the set of chronological formulas. Moreover,
let Fchron,m be the set of chronological formulas with m free variables x1, . . . , xm.

Definition 3.7 (Shift on formulas). For t0 ∈[0,∞), we define a shift operation St0 on Lproc-formulas

(1) For a basic formula φ, we obtain St0φ by replacing each occurrence of a constant symbol zt,t′,j
by zt0+t,t0+t′,j.
(2) For a continuous connective f ∶Rk →R, we set St0[f(φ1,...,φk)] = f(St0φ1,...,St0φk).

Lemma 3.8. The shift operators satisfy St0St1 = St0+t1.
Proof. We proceed by induction on the complexity of the formulas.
Consider the case of a basic
formula φ. To obtain St1φ, we replace zt,t′,j by zt1+t,t1+t′,j. Then applying St0 will replace zt1+t,t1+t′,j

The case of a continuous connective is immediate.
For the case of a sup quantifier, observe that if φ satisfies the induction hypothesis, then

With the aid of the shift operation, we can give an alternative procedure for generating chrono-
logical formulas, which is useful for inductive arguments later in the paper.

Definition 3.11. If T is a theory in some metric language (e.g. Lproc) and φ and ψ are formulas, we
say that φ and ψ are equivalent modulo T if φM = ψM for every M satisfying T. Similarly, we say
that two collections of formulas are equivalent modulo T if every formula in one of the collections is
equivalent to a formula in the other collection.
Lemma 3.12. Let S be the set of formulas in Lproc generated recursively as follows:
(1) Every basic formula without the symbols zs,t (i.e. basic formula in Lfilt) is in S.
(2) If φ1, . . . , φk ∈S and f ∶Rk →R is continuous, then f(φ1,...,φk) ∈S.
(3) If φ(x,y) is in S and r > 0, then supy∈D0,r φ(x,y) and infy∈D0,r φ(x,y) are in S.
(4) If φ(x,y1,...,yk) ∈S and j1, . . . , jk ∈N and t > 0, then (Stφ)(x,z0,t,j1,...,z0,t,jk) is in S.
Then S is equivalent to Fchron modulo Tproc.
Proof. First, we prove that S ⊆Fchron. It suffices to show that Lchron is closed under the operations
(1) - (4). In fact, closure under (1) - (3) is clear from Definition 3.6. For (4), it suffices to show that
Lchron is closed under shifts and under substituting one of the constant symbols z0,t for one of the free
variables. The closure of Lchron under shifts follows from an inductive argument based on Definition
3.6; in fact, one can show that if φ ∈Fchron(s), then Stφ ∈Fchron(s + t). Similarly, an inductive
argument on Definition 3.6 shows that Fchron(t) is closed under the operation of substituting one of
the constant symbols for one of the free variables.
Second, we prove that Fchron ⊆S modulo Tproc. In fact, we prove the following claim: If φ(x) ∈
Lchron(t), then
φ(x) = (Stψ)(x,zs1,t1,i1,...,zsn,tn,in)
(3.1)
for some n ∈N0 and ψ(x,y1,...,yn) ∈S and 0 ≤sj ≤tj ≤t for j = 1,...,n. We handle each of the
cases in Definition 3.6:
(1) A basic formula in Lproc is a composition of predicate symbols with terms. By an induction
argument, each Lproc-term can be expressed as an Lfilt term composed with the constant symbols
(zs,t)0≤s≤t. Hence, every basic formula has the form φ(x) = ψ(x,zs1,t1,i1,...,zsn,tn,in) where φ is a
basic formula in Lfilt.
Now we can show that φ ∈S. Let {τ1,...,τk} = {s1,...,sn,t1,...,tn} as sets with τ1 < ⋅⋅⋅< τk.

Remark 3.18. This example has the following application for the model theory of von Neumann alge-
bras. Let L(F∞) be the free group von Neumann algebra on infinitely many generators, which is well
known to be isomorphic to the von Neumann algebra M generated by a free Brownian motion above.
Then the first-factor embedding of L(F∞) into L(F∞) ∗L(F∞) is an elementary embedding. Indeed,
this inclusion is isomorphic to the inclusion of M0 into Mt0 discussed above, which is elementary as a
special case of stationarity (Lemma 3.16).
Remark 3.19. Various natural properties of a NC filtration and stochastic process are chronologically
axiomatizable (i.e. axiomatizable using chronological formulas). In the following, we already restrict
our attention to structures M satisfying Lproc.
• We say that M has the martingale property if for each s ≤t and j ∈N, we have EMs[zs,t,j] = 0.
This property is axiomatizable using the conditions

for noncommutative polynomials p1, . . . , pm and m ∈N. The above expression, again, can be
expanded and expressed in terms of the real and imaginary parts of traces of polynomials in zs,t
and x1, . . . , xm.
Similarly, one can axiomatize (with quantifier-free sentences) the property that the increments zs,t
have real and imaginary parts which form a free semicircular family with variance t −s.
3.3
The restriction to M0; definable predicates and functions
In this subsection, we consider the restriction of chronological formulas to the initial element of the
filtration M0 as defining a language Lchron,0 which extends Ltr.
While this might appear to be
merely a semantic exercise, it is useful because it will allow us to apply results about types, definable
predicates, and definable functions over metric structures directly to the chronological versions, rather
than repeating the proofs.
If we only have access to M0, then we would only be able to apply suprema and infima associated
to D0,r for r ≥0, while the suprema and infima associated to Dt,r for t > 0 cannot be expressed in
terms of M0. We therefore have to artificially add in predicates in Lchron,0 which represent general
chronological formulas. This is essentially the same as the procedure of Morleyization in model theory,
in which a new language is defined with relation symbols for every formula in the original language; in
particular, the formulas in the original language are all quantifier-free formulas in the new language.
We will then discuss several theories and axioms in the new language Lchron,0 related to chronological
theories in Lproc.
26


## Page 27


Definition 3.20. We define Lchron,0 as follows:
• The domains are (Dr)r>0 as in Ltr the language of tracial von Neumann algebras.
• The function symbols describe the ∗-algebra operations, the same as in Ltr.
• The predicate symbols are indexed by the chronological formulas φ in Lproc; for clarity, we
denote the corresponding predicate in Lchron,0 by Θ(φ).
Note that a more common notation for Θ(φ) in the model theory literature is Pφ.
Definition 3.21. Given an Lchron-theory T, we define an Lchron,0-theory Θ0(T) as follows:
(1) For each Lchron-sentence φ ∈T, we have Θ(φ) ∈Θ(T).
(2) For each Lchron-formula φ in k free variables and for terms t1, . . . , tk in variables (x1,...,xm),
we have

...

∣Θ(φ)(t1(x),...,tk(x)) −Θ(φ(t1,...,tm))(x)∣= 0.

sup

...

and the same with supy∈D0,r and supy∈Dr replaced by infy∈D0,r and infy∈Dr respectively.
(4) For each f ∶Rk →R is continuous and φ1, . . . , φk are Lchron-formulas in m free variables, we have
sup

...

∣f(Θ(φ1),...,Θ(φk)) −Θ(f(φ1,...,φk))∣∈Θ(T).
Remark 3.22. Intuitively, these axioms are saying that the map φ ↦Θ(φ) respects composition
with terms, connectives, and the supremum operation if we understand the underlying space for the
Lchron,0-structure to be 0th level of the filtration in the Lproc-structure. Hence, the next lemma should
not be surprising.
Lemma 3.23. Let T be an Lchron theory. Then every formula in Lchron,0 is equivalent modulo Θ0(T)
to a formula of the form Θ(φ) for some Lchron-formula φ.
Proof. We proceed by induction on the complexity of formulas.
(1) Suppose that φ is a basic formula in Lchron,0. Then φ has the form Θ(ψ)(t1,...,tk) for some
predicate symbol Θ(ψ) and terms t1, . . . , tk in light of Definition 3.21 (1). By Definition 3.21 (2),
φ is equivalent to Θ(ψ(t1,...,tk)).
(2) Suppose that φ(x) = supy∈Dr ψ(x,y). By inductive hypothesis, assume ψ is equivalent to Θ(ψ′)
for some ψ′ ∈Lchron. Let φ′ = supy∈D0,r ψ′(x,y), which is in Lchron. Then using Definition 3.21
(3), φ is equivalent to Θ(φ′).
(3) Suppose that φ = f(φ1,...,φk) where f is a continuous connective. Then applying the inductive
hypothesis to φ1, . . . , φk and using Definition 3.21, we obtain that φ is equivalent to Θ(φ′) for
some φ′.
Lemma 3.24. Suppose that M is an Lfilt-structure satisfying a chronological theory T. Let M0 be
the Lchron,0-structure obtained as follows:
• Dr in Lchron,0 is interpreted as DM
0,r.
27


## Page 28


• The function symbols in M0 are the restrictions of the function symbols from M.
• For φ ∈Lchron, Θ(φ) is interpreted as φM restricted to inputs in ⋃r>0 DM0
r
.
Then M0 satisfies Θ0(T).
Proof. The result follows by straightforwardly checking that M0 satisfies each of the kinds of sentences
in Θ0(T). For instance the sentences of type (1) hold because for each sentence φ ∈T, we have that
Θ(φ)M0 = φM by definition.
For type (2), this is because the function symbols in M0 are the
restrictions of those in M. Cases (3) and (4) follow similarly.
Definition 3.25. Let T be a Lchron-theory. Define Θ1(T) as the set of Lchron,0-sentences satisfied by
M0 by M0 for all Lfilt-structures satisfying T.
Remark 3.26. Note it is immediate from Lemma 3.24 that Θ1(T) implies Θ0(T), and hence also that
every Lchron,0-formula is equivalent to a formula of the form Θ(φ). Such a formula is, by construction,
considered a quantifier-free formula in Lchron,0.
Remark 3.27. Θ1(T) can also be described as follows. If we view complete theories as linear functionals
as in [31, Lemma 3.8ff], the mapping that sends Th(M) to Th(M0) for Lfilt-structures M is a
restriction mapping and hence is continuous. Thus, the image of the restriction map gives a closed
set of complete Lchron,0-theories, which corresponds to an axiomatizable class of Lchron,0-structures.
Then Θ1(T) is the complete theory of this axiomatizable class. From this reasoning, it follows that
every consistant complete theory extending Θ(T) can be realized by the restriction M0 for some M
satisfying T.
With these preparations in hand, we set up the definitions and basic facts for chronological definable
predicates, definable functions, and types.
Definition 3.28 (Chronologically definable predicates). Let T be an Lchron-theory. The space of
chronologically definable predicates in m free variables in M0 is the completion of Fchron,m with respect
to the family of seminorms ∥⋅∥0,r,T for r ∈(0,∞).
The following fact is now straightforward to verify from the setup.
Lemma 3.29. For Lchron formulas in m-free variables, we have ∥φ∥0,r,T = ∥Θ(φ)∥r,Θ1(T). Hence, the
space of chronologically definable predicates with inputs in M0 is isomorphic as a topological vector
space to the space of definable predicates in m free variables in Lchron,0 with respect to Θ1(T).
Definition 3.30 (Chronological types).
(1) Let M be an Lfilt-structure and x ∈Mm

(3) The space of chronological types is Schron,m(T) = ⋃r∈(0,∞)m Schron,r(T) equipped with the inductive
limit topology.
Definition 3.31. For an Lchron,0-theory T, and r ∈(0,∞)m, let Sr(T) denote the space of types of
m-tuples in D0,r, and similarly Sm(T) the space of types of all m-tuples.
Lemma 3.32. Let T be an Lchron-theory.
The map Θ from Fchron,m to Lchron,0-formulas in m
variables induces homeomorphisms Schron,r(T) →Sr(Θ1(T)) and Schron,m(T) →Sm(Θ1(T)).
Corollary 3.33. There is an isomorphism of topological vector spaces from the space of chronologically
definable predicates with respect to T to C(Sm(T)), such that if fφ is the function corresponding to
the definable predicate φ, then ∥φ∥0,r,T = ∥fφ∣Sm(T)∥u.

## Page 29


Proof. It is standard that the space of definable predicates with respect to Lchron,0 and Θ1(T) is
isomorphic to the continuous functions on Sm(Θ1(T)) (see [43, Proposition 3.9] for the setting with
domains of quantification used here). We then apply Lemmas 3.29 and 3.32 to transform this into a
statement about the spaces of chronological definable predicates and types.
Definition 3.34 (Chronologically definable functions). Let T be an Lchron-theory. A chronologically
definable function in m variables is a collection of functions fM ∶(M0)m →M0 for models M of T
such that
(1) For every r > 0, there exists r′ > 0 such that f M maps DM
0,r into DM
0,r′ for every M satisfying T.
(2) There exists a chronologically definable predicate φ relative to T such that dM(f M(x),y) =
φM(x,y) for all M satisfying T and all x ∈Mm
0 and y ∈M0.
Remark 3.35. Although the class of models of T is not a set, it is sufficient for f M to be given for a
single M in each elementary equivalence class for T that satisfies (2) for the same φ. Indeed, it is clear
that for each M, f M is definable over M as in [9, Definition 2.22] and thus by [9, Definition 2.25], fM
admits a canonical extension in any elementary extension N of M, and of course the same definable
predicate φ satisfies (2) in N. Any M′ ≡M can be elementarily embedded into some elementary
extension N of M (this is an easier form of the Keisler–Shelah theorem; see [9, Theorem 5.7 and
Proposition 7.10]), and fN will leave an elementary submodel invariant, and so we obtain fM′ as
the restriction. But then we note that by the downward L¨owenheim–Skolem theorem [9, Proposition
7.3], each elementary equivalence class has a representative with a fixed bound on its cardinality
that only depends on the language. In conclusion, by specifying fM only for set of equivalence class
representatives, we see that the class of definable functions is indeed a set.
Lemma 3.36. Let T be an Lchron-theory and fix m ∈N. There is a bijection Θ from chronologically
definable functions in m-variables with respect to T and definable functions with respect to Θ1(T) such
that for each M satisfying T, we have Θ(f)M0 = f M as functions on M0.
Proof. For each chronologically definable f, suppose φ is a corresponding chronologically definable
predicate in Definition 3.34 (2). Now Θ(f)M0 can defined as f M when M0 is the restriction of an Lfilt-
structure M satisfying T. By Remark 3.27, every N satisfying Θ1(T) is elementarily equivalent to
such an M0, so, as in Remark 3.35, Θ(f)N can be canonically defined for any N satisfying Θ1(T).
Corollary 3.37. Let f = (f1,...,fm2) be a tuple of chronologically definable functions in m1 variables,
and let g = (g1,...,gm3) be a tuple of chronologically definable functions in m2 variables. Then g ○f
is a tuple of chronologically definable functions.
Similarly, if φ is a chronologically definable predicate in m2 variables, then φ○f is a chronologically
definable predicate.
Proof. This follows by rewriting the statement in terms of Θ1(T) and Lchron,0 and then applying
the known results about definable functions and definable predicates. See e.g. [43, Definition 3.16,
Proposition 3.17, Observation 3.21].
We conclude the section by showing that the space of chronologically definable predicates is sepa-
rable.
Lemma 3.38 (Separability of chronologically definable predicates). Fix m ∈N ∪{∞}. The space
of chronological definable predicates with repsect to Tstat is separable with respect to the family of
seminorms ∥⋅∥0,r,Tstat for r ∈(0,∞)m. In particular, Schron,r(Tstat) is metrizable in the weak-∗topology
for each r.

4
Free Brownian motion in the matrix ultraproduct
In this section, we begin to relate chronological formulas with random matrices. In particular, we
want to obtain a filtration of von Neumann algebras and a free Brownian motion as an ultraproduct
of the Brownian motions in n × n matrices (compare [17]).

one can assume that G0 is a σ-algebra associated to an atomless (or diffuse) probability space measure

Then (L∞(Ω,Gt,P) ⊗Mn)t∈[0,∞]) is a filtration of von Neumann algebras with the trace given
by E ⊗trn. However, as usual in the study of asymptotic freeness, we want to study almost sure
convergence of traces rather than merely convergence of the expected trace. We thus want to be able
to interpret formulas as pointwise functions on Mn.
While [46] simply evaluated Ltr-formulas on Mn pointwise, this is not an option in our setting
because our filtration is not defined on Mn itself, but rather inherently uses the classical randomness.
Instead, we use the idea of center-valued evaluation of formulas from [31], where formulas are inter-
preted as functions with inputs in the von Neumann algebra and outputs in the center, rather than
the real numbers. In particular for L∞(Ω,G,P) ⊗Mn, this produces a function in L∞(Ω,G,P).
We study the behavior of these center-valued formulas in the We take a quotient π ∶M →Q by
a maximal ideal, which produces a II1-factor Q elementarily equivalent to the matrix ultraproduct
∏n→U Mn. Following [31, §6], this can be viewed as a kind of “derandomization”; rather than selecting
a point ωn in Ωfor each n, we perform the ultraproduct first and then take the quotient that removes
the center.
The quotients Qt = π(Mt) provide a filtration of II1 factors with an associated free
Brownian motion zs,t,j obtained as the ultraproduct of the matrix Brownian motions.
A key point in making this construction work is to show that the center-valued evaluation of a
chronological formula φ in the random matrix ultraproduct can be approximated asymptotically by
a pointwise function Λ(n)
φ
∶(Mn)m →Mn (see Proposition 4.13). The functions Λ(n)
φ
will also be
crucial for our definition of entropy for chronological types in §5.1, and allows us to connect classical
stochastic optimization problems with their noncommutative counterparts in §5.2.

We now want to adapt the results of [31, §3] for the setting of Lchron rather than Ltr. Most of the
proofs work in the exact same way, but they require an important hypothesis.
Definition 4.3 (Chronological invariance property). Let (Mt)t∈[0,∞] be a filtration of von Neumann
algebras and let z = (zs,t,j)0≤s≤t<∞,j∈N be elements zs,t,j ∈DM
0,6

the chronological invariance property if whenever φ ∈Fchron(t) is a formula with m free variables, we
have

The chronological invariance property is somewhat similar to various notions of adaptedness in
classical and noncommutative probability (compare for instance [50, Definition 6.2(iv)-(v)]).
We
will show in the next section that the random matrix ultraproducts (4.1) satisfy the chronological
invariance property.
It is natural to ask for general criteria to test the chronological invariance
property, but we leave this question for future research.
The key point in [31, §3] is the behavior of the N-valued interpretation of formulas with respect
to partitioning by projection-valued measures. Recall that a projection-valued measure (PVM) over
M is a family (ej)j∈J of projections in M such that ∑j∈J ej = 1 in the strong operator topology. (Of
course, projection-valued measures in the sense of the spectral theorem are generally defined over a
measure space, rather than a discrete index set. The terminology here is borrowed from the quantum
setting, but can be regarded as a special case of projection-valued measures in the functional-analytic
sense.) In adapting [31, Lemma 3.14], we need to assume that the PVM takes values in N ∩Mt when
the formula is in Fchron(t), which is what will lead us to the chronological invariance property as a
hypothesis in the later statements (see Proposition 4.5).

We now recall the “ultrafiber” construction from [31] (taken essentially from [8]). Given N ⊆Z(M),
we take a quotient of M by a maximal ideal corresponding to a character (or equivalently, a pure
state) on N. We can then relate the N-valued interpretations of formulas in M to the plain evaluation
of formulas on the quotient by [8, Theorem 3.19] or [31, Theorem 3.18], which is an analog of  Lo´s’s
theorem for ultraproducts. Our present goal is to adapt this result to the setting of filtrations. First,
we recall the definition of the ultrafiber.
Definition 4.6 ([31, Definition 2.1]). Let M be a finite von Neumann algebra, N ⊆Z(M) be a
subalgebra of its center, and E ∶M →N be a normal, faithful, tracial conditional expectation. Let V
be a character on N, and let
IE,V = {x ∈M ∶V ○E[x∗x] = 0}.
Define M /E,V = M/IE,V and let π be the quotient map. Let τE,V = V ○E viewed as a state on M /E,V.
It is shown in [31, Proposition 2.2] that M /E,V is a von Neumann algebra and τE,V is a faithful
normal tracial state. We then have the following result that adapts [31, Theorem 3.18] to the setting
of filtrations.

5
Entropy for chronological types
5.1
Definition of entropy
Next, we define the version of free entropy using chronological formulas. First, note that unlike [44],
we cannot directly phrase the definition in terms of open neighborhoods in the type space. Indeed,
the chronological formulas do not have a relevant interpretation in Mn because our filtration is on
L∞(Ω,F,P)⊗Mn rather than Mn itself. We will in fact define the microstate spaces using the functions
Λ(n)
φ
from Proposition 4.13 (and verify below that the definition is independent of the different choices
of Λ(n)
φ
as in Remark 4.14).
For convenience, we define the entropy using the Gaussian Ginibre probability measure on Mm
n ,
which we denote by σ(n), rather than Lebesgue measure as the background measure similar to [11],
and the sign is changed so that the resulting quantity is nonnegative. We include a tilde to distinguish
this from the version with Lebesgue measure. We also add the subscript chron to indicate the use of
chronological formulas. We write the definition in terms of some Lproc-structure M satisfying Tproc,
but as we will see in Remark 5.11 below, it is only nontrivial in the case that M is chronologically
elementarily equivalent to the random matrix ultraproduct quotient structure Q discussed in §4.3.
Hence, for most purposes, the reader can always assume that tuples x and y are from Q.

Now that we know that ˜χU

can make the following definition.
Definition 5.12 (Conditional chronological entropy: final version). For a tuple (x,y) in M0 for an
Lproc-structure M satisfying Tproc, if there exists Y(n) satisfying (5.13), we define
˜χU
chron(x ∣y) = ˜χU
chron(x ∣Y(n) ↝y),
or equivalently, define ˜χU
chron by the right-hand side of (5.15). In case that no Y(n) exists satisfying
(5.13), we set ˜χU
chron(x ∣y) = +∞.
Remark 5.13. We remark that if Y(n) is some matrix tuple that does not satisfy (5.13), then ˜χU
chron(x ∣
Y(n) ↝y) will be infinite, because for a sufficiently precise choice of Φ and ε, the microstate space
Γ(n)(x ∣Y(n) ↝y;Φ,ε) will be empty. Hence, it is natural to define the entropy to be infinite in this
case. Similarly, note that if (x,y) does not admit any matrix approximations, then ˜χU
chron(x ∣y) will
always be +∞.

5.3
Chain rule
In this section, we prove a chain rule for ˜χU
chron under iterated conditioning. The corresponding result
for classical entropy of course is well-known. The main difficulty in establishing such a result in the
free probability setting was that there was no analog of Theorem 5.10 which would show that the
entropy of x conditioned on y is independent of the choice of matrix approximations Y(n) for y.
